BOARD OF SUPERVISORS:
 
a. Presentation to Snowflake High School Track Team (girls & boys) for
winning the Division 4 Track and Field State Championship:
Chairman Jason Whiting congratulated the boys and girls team on their
state championships. 
The Coach stated that they had an excellent team, that this is the fourth
year in a row that the boys team won state and the second year in a row for
the girls team to win state. She stated that the girls team won with 146
points, more than doubling the second-place team, indicating that they
have a very strong program.  She further stated that the boys’ team won by
22 points and spoke of the strong performance they gave to be able to take
first place, indicating that they are proud of the boys’ team for coming
together and getting the win.  She indicated that they had fifteen kids qualify
for state and that all of the girls relay teams won state.  She shared how
proud she is of the kids, indicating that they are all great kids and athletes. 
She spoke about the athletes that set school records.  She expressed her
appreciation to the excellent coaching staff. 
Chairman Whiting congratulated the team, indicating that it was a lot of
work and determination to accomplish this.  He thanked the coaches.  He
stated that this is much more than an individual accomplishment, that it’s a
group coming together, united in purpose and being able to accomplish
something pretty amazing.  He indicated that he hopes they can take the
things they have learned and use them in their lives.  He wished them
continued success. He presented the teams with medals in recognition of
  
                                                                    2 of 18

continued success. He presented the teams with medals in recognition of
their accomplishment.   
 
b. Presentation to the Joseph City High School Softball Team for winning the
2024 State Championship: 
Supervisor Alberto Peshlakai recognized the team on the achievement,
indicating that they are joining in the celebration with their community.    
Coach Daniel Hutchins spoke about the season, indicating that they put in a
lot of hard work, including working in the summer and off season. He
indicated that they only lost two regular season games, making it a season
for the books.  He stated that they qualified as the #2 seed for the state
tournament, that they won the first two games pretty easily but that the third
was a little closer.  He spoke of the championship game, indicating that
they scored 26 runs.  He stated that for the four games played in the state
tournament they scored a total of 61 runs and 15 home runs.  He spoke of
many of the players making All-Region and All-State and one player being
Player of the Year in their conference.  He stated that they had an amazing
season, that they played as a team and worked together.  He indicated that
they ended their season on a 21-game winning streak and that they haven’t
lost a home game in two years.  He shared his appreciation to the coaching
staff.    
Supervisor Peshlakai stated that the hard work, teamwork, and dedication
paid off.  He spoke   He stated that the Board of Supervisors join with the
community and school in their celebration.  He congratulated the team and
presented them with medals in recognition of their accomplishment.
 
  
c. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and adoption of Resolution Number
19-24, deannexing territory from the Town of Pinetop-Lakeside and
annexing the same territory to the City of Show Low:
Jason Moore stated that the property is approximately sixty-acres near
Show Low Lake.  He indicated that the cities have worked out an
agreement where Pinetop will deannex the sixty-acres and Show Low will
annex within their city limits.  He indicated that both cities have adopted
appropriate ordinances, that are included in the board materials, and that
they have followed the proper procedures under 9-471.02.  He stated that
the County’s role is very limited and would be to hear any protests that
were filed, but that he is not aware of any protests that have been filed.  He
stated that staff would recommend adoption of the proposed resolution. 
Chairman Whiting opened the meeting for public comments.  There being
no comments, the public hearing was closed.
Supervisor Seymore inquired if it is correct that it would be difficult for
Pinetop-Lakeside to provide things such as sewer and water to the property
but that the City of Show Low would be able to do so to allow for
development.
Anna Atencio, Attorney for the City of Show Low, indicated that that is
correct, that it is surrounded by the City of Show Low and that the city would
be able to provide infrastructure to the area, as well as the developer
wanting to have one jurisdiction for law enforcement for the entire
development.   
  
                                                                    3 of 18

b. Presentation/Summary of Navajo County’s Fiscal Year 2023 Annual
Financial Report, Report on Internal Control and on Compliance, and
Federal Single Audit Report:
Jayson Vowell introduced the auditors, Don Bohart and Nikolas
Rabayda, and stated that as required by statute, the auditors are required
to come to the Board and present the financial status of the financial
reporting.    
A presentation was shown. Don Bohart, Audit Manager on the Fiscal Year
2023 audits, spoke about the Auditor General’s Office and what they do. 
He stated that they will be discussing three required annual reports, the
Annual Comprehensive Financial Report, the Report on Internal Control
and Compliance and the Federal Single Audit Report.  He indicated that for
the first report, the County’s Annual Comprehensive Financial Report,
where the audit objective is to obtain reasonable assurance about the
financial statements as a whole are free from material misstatements due
to fraud or error and to issue a report that includes their opinion, for FY23
they have reported an unmodified or clean opinion, which means the
County’s financial statements are reliable.  He stated the second report on
Internal Control and Compliance is where they report any findings and
  
                                                                    4 of 18 
DRAFT

<!-- page 10 -->
Internal Control and Compliance is where they report any findings and
recommendations results from the audit of the County’s Financial
Statements, indicating that three findings were reported, which they will
review in detail.  He indicated that the third report, the Federal Single Aduit
Report, includes their assessment of the County’s compliance with federal
program revenues, the requirements over each federal program, and the
County’s schedule of federal expenditures, they reported one finding, which
will be discussed in detail.  He stated that reasonable assurance is a high
but not absolute level of assurance, that the audits are performed following
governmental auditing standards and federal uniform guidance but that
there’s no guarantee that they will always detect material misstatements or
non-compliance.  He indicated that there was no fraud identified in the
FY23 reports of the County.  He spoke about the highlights that were
included, including the County’s largest primary revenue source and how
the County used the money over the past ten years.   He reviewed the main
sources of revenue, including federal and state grants and programs,
shared state sales taxes, county sales taxes and county property taxes and
spoke about the changes in those over the past five years.  He further
reviewed the four primary expenses, including general government, public
safety, highways and streets and health and welfare and talked about the
changes in those over the past five years.  He stated that the County’s
overall net position increased by $10.9 million is FY23, noting that not all of
the net position is spendable at the end of FY23 due to investments in
capital assets and restrictions for specific uses such as highways/streets
and medical costs, resulting in a negative or deficit unrestricted balance
due to the county’s pension and OPEB obligations.  He spoke about the
highlights for the federal expenditures for the past five years, indicating that
most are from the Department of Treasury and Department of Health &
Human Services as a result of COVID grants and that while the county
continues to receive some money associated with COVID it is substantially
less that prior years. 
Nicholas Rabayda noted that the County has corrected four of the reported
findings from the previous fiscal year and that the County’s FY22 initial
financial statement submitted for audit consideration contained a material
misstatement but that they didn’t identify any material misstatements during
the FY23 audit.  He stated that in the report on Internal Controls and
Compliance, they reported three financial statement findings in the report
as follows.  The first finding was regarding deficiencies in the County’s
purchasing fuel card process, and an unallowable alcoholic beverage
purchase, which has since been reimbursed.  He reviewed the
recommendations including that the county ensure that all employees
follow county policies by improving its procedures over purchasing card fuel
purchases, conduct an in-depth review of employees prior to fuel
purchases, and follow existing county policy which requires the purchasing
card approver to review all transactions and investigate and report misuse. 
He stated that the County concurs with the finding and plans to implement
the recommendations by June 30, 2024.  The second finding identified
deficiencies in the county process for risk management and documenting
its information technology risks, which includes identifying, analyzing and
respond to the county-wide information or IT risks and not including
classifying and inventory sensitive information that may need stronger
                                                                    5 of 18 
DRAFT

<!-- page 11 -->
access and security controls.  He reviewed the recommendation including
a plan for where to allocate resources and where to implement critical
controls, perform an annual entity-wide IT risk assessment that includes
evaluating and documenting risks and safeguards, and evaluate and
manage the risks of holding sensitive information by classifying and
inventorying information.  The third finding identified deficiencies in the
County’s control over its systems and data.  He stated that they
recommend that the county continues to implement comprehensive IT
policies and procedures and develop process to ensure the procedures
being constantly followed to restrict access to its IT system and manage
system configurations and changes.  He stated that the County concurs
with both of those findings and plans to fully correct them by June 30,
2025.  As to the Single Audit Report, he stated that they audited four
federal programs, and indicated that as to one of the programs they
reported a federal compliance finding related to WIOA.  He reviewed the
finding, stating that the department failed to ensure that it spent the
required twenty percent of program dollars that were earmarked to provide
in school and out of school youth paid and unpaid work experience during
the period of April 2021 to June 2023. He stated that they recommendation
is that the department spend no less than the required twenty percent of
the youth activities money on providing youth with paid and unpaid work
experience, implement strategies to recruit and retain in-school and
out-of-school youth, monitor both the county and its subrecipients paid and
unpaid work experience throughout the award period and adjust spending
to meet the earmarked requirement if work experience participation is lower
than expected.  
Mr. Vowell stated that Navajo County was the first to issue their financial
report for FY23 and that out of fifteen counties only two counties made the
deadline.  He stated that the financial report is a consolidated financial
report that is submitted to the Government Finance Officers Association for
their Certificate of Achievement Program, indicating that they have done
this consistently over the years and been awarded that.   He shared his
appreciation to the Auditors General Office for being able to work with
them, indicating that it is helpful for being able to identify opportunities for
improvement.   
Chairman Whiting thanked the Auditors for the partnership and talked
about the importance of having the audit.  He referred to the comment
made regarding fraud not being found but indicated that the findings are to
help prevent mis happenings from being able to occur.  He spoke of the
importance for the continued teamwork and collaboration to try and find
solutions so that they can make sure they are doing everything correctly
and that it sounds like there are plans in place to make sure it continues to
move forward in a positive way.

SHERIFF'S OFFICE:
 
a. Presentation on the Reaching Rural Project:
A presentation was shown. Dawn Wilson spoke about the Reaching Rural
initiative, indicating that it is a one-year initiative that brought together
different rural practitioners of different backgrounds and communities
throughout the country to support in advancing solutions to address the
challenges of substance abuse in the communities.  She indicated that in
November 2022 they selected sixty-seven fellows from twenty-six counties
across the United States and that Navajo County was one of the counties
selected.  She spoke about the team that was put together including
Supervisor Daryl Seymore, Lieutenant Lea Peterson, Alyssa Lemmons,
Lonna Young and herself.  She talked stated that throughout the year their
team met monthly and also went to different locations in the United States
to learn about the programs other locations had.  She stated that they
looked at the challenges both locally and throughout the region to identify
opportunities to serve individuals with substance abuse and co-occurring
disorders more effectively.  She spoke about the background information
that they based their application on, including overdose fatalities within the
county and rural transportation issues.  She indicated that together they
determined that they would create a jail-based peer recovery support
service program that could reach those individuals during incarceration,
with the hope that they could impact them as they transitioned back into the
community.  She stated that they know the program will help strengthen
partnerships with peer organizations, public safety, public health, local
elected officials and other community organizations by addressing the
substance abuse disorder locally.      
Alyssa Lemmons talked about the goals for the project, including serving
justice involved individuals with co-occurring substance use and behavioral
health disorders, reaching twenty inmates at a time through the peer
recovery support services, and enhance reentry services and build a peer
network for incarcerated community members including tribal members and
veterans.  She spoke about the plans and implementation for the project,
indicating it will be a two-year project for the first phase. 
Lea Peterson stated that they have inmate come back over and over again
and that they were not receiving any help.  She spoke of the goals to

REGULAR AGENDA:
 
1. Presentation regarding the current wildfire conditions, restrictions status
and preparedness information:
A presentation was shown. Catrina Jenkins stated that they use the
outlooks each year as a tool and talked about the outlook over the next few
months, indicating that wildfire is always a threat when you live in the
wildland urban interface.  She stated that the White Mountain Fire
Restrictions Coordination Group is made up on coordinating agencies from
various levels across the region, that there are subject matter experts on
the committee that evaluated data-based information each week and the
science provided data provides the information of whether or not they will
go into fire restrictions.  She spoke about the Ready, Set, Go program,
indicating that Ready, Set, Go is the language all fifteen counties have
adopted that goes into preparedness information as well as evacuations. 
She stated that you can be prepared by having your alert system sign up
completed, having your property Firewise and defensible space made and
  
                                                                    9 of 18 
DRAFT

<!-- page 15 -->
that your communications, evacuation, and financial emergency plans are
up to date.  She indicated that everybody needing to be signed up for the
Navajo County Sheriffs Office app and the Ready Navajo County Alert
System, indicating that these are areas where you can get vetted official
information about incidents and emergencies in and around Navajo
County.  She talked about the Set stage, indicating that this is when there is
something going on in your neighborhood and you are paying attention to
the vetted sources of information, that you have your kit to your door or
vehicle and if extra time is necessary, you could leave the area if you think
there will be an evacuation.  She stated if an evacuation is necessary, they
send the Go message, indicating that you want to leave immediately
because you may not get a second alert and that it’s important to follow the
instructions received when you get this alert.  She stated that if someone
chooses not to leave, they will not be allowed to leave the property for any
reason and that emergency services may not be available.   She spoke
about how most wildfires are created by humans and the ways to prevent
starting a wildfire, such as using an ashtray when smoking, not dragging
chains, maintaining the undercarriage of your vehicle, and putting out
campfires.  She spoke about creating a defensible space by cleaning out
getters and rooftops and putting reflective numbers on your house.       
Chief Deputy Brian Swanty stated that this is a non-stop process each
year.  He talked about stated that Ms. Jenkins does a great job each year
getting everyone organized and scheduling the weekly meetings to discuss
fire restrictions and that they have a fantastic team when it comes to wildfire
season.  He stated that communication that is relayed to the public is
critical and that many people get bad information that is not vetted, that
there is a Public Information Group that shares information on different
sites and recommended that citizens get the information from a vetted
source. 
Chief Loney, with Timber Mesa Fire, talked about the area that Timber
Mesa covers and how many residents they service.  He stated that they
have a strong relationship and collaborative efforts with local partners.  He
stated that wildland fire still remains the number one threat to the life and
property of the people in the White Mountains.  He talked about not
becoming complacent and challenged community members to engage in
proactive efforts to be prepared.  He talked about being Firewise, and about
having a defensible area around your house.  He stated that they will be
holding the White Mountain Communities Annual Firewise Block Party on
Saturday, July 13, 2024, from 10:00 a.m. to 3:00 p.m. at the Blue Ridge
Elementary School. 
JD Pepper, Division Chief Fire Marshall Timber Meas Fire District, shared
his appreciation to Navajo County Emergency Management, Navajo
County Sheriff’s Office and Planning and Zoning and talked about the
cooperation efforts with each of the departments.  He stated that his job is
to assess threats to the public and communities and to respond to and
mitigate those threats.  He stated that they recently conducted some Basal
Scoring Tests, which is a way or scoring the density of woody biomass,
within the City of Show Low and talked about the results as well as
mitigation efforts that can be taken. 
Randy Chevalier, Fire Chief for Timber Mesa, thanked the board for support
from the County on a regular basis.  He stated that wildfires are a growing
                                                                    10 of 18 
DRAFT

<!-- page 16 -->
problem and that everyone is going to have to band together to figure out
how to solve it.  He about wildfire risks in their area and risks that occur at
citizens houses that create challenges for emergency response teams.
Supervisor Seymore spoke about how everyone has been effected by fire
risks and increase in insurance premiums due to the fire risks.  He stated
that we each need to do what we can because of the amount it costs.  He
stated that Emergency Management does have grants to help mitigate
citizens property, so it is not all out of pocket.    
Ms. Jenkins stated that the Grant Coordinator in her office takes care of the
Health Forest Initiative Grants, that they go out and do an assessment and
that citizens can get on the grant list if they choose to and that homeowners
can be reimbursed a percentage of the cost, they pay to have mitigation
work done. 
Supervisor Whitesinger inquired if citizens would call different
organizations for this grant depending on where they lived.   
Ms. Jenkins stated that they can call any of the organizations, that they just

for the grant reimbursement.  She stated that some of the agency partners
may be restricted to areas but that they County is not.
Chief Pepper stated that they all have Firewise assessors and that any of
them can do the assessment.  He talked about the calls they get regarding
citizens having issues getting insured due to where they live.  He spoke
about getting the Firewise assessment and getting the work done so they
can provide the information to the insurance company.  He talked about
working with the City of Show Low in the future to hopefully get a code
adoption for fire adaptive homes.  He spoke of the challenges that are

Supervisor Whitesinger inquired if once your property is Firewise there is
something that can be provided to your insurance company.  
Chief Chevalier talked about being Firewise approved and still having

won’t be a short-term fix, that it will be a long process.  He stated that you
still need to make the effort to be fire wise and to help your neighbors as
well. 
Supervisor Whitesinger thanked them for their work on the initiative.  She

Chairman Whiting spoke about the insurance companies, indicating they

appreciation to them for trying to bridge the gap and help them understand. 
He stated that industry has gone into those areas and started to clean up
and that there has been significant work done and that now it’s a matter of
getting the insurance companies to model the work being done.  He
thanked them for presenting the information.

William Flake stated that this contract is for a pavement preservation

improve road conditions and the public safety for traveling on the road.  He
talked about the bid process.  He stated that staff recommends approval to
Sunland Asphalt and Construction.  
Supervisor Seymore talked about the three bids received, two being local
bidders and the other not being local, and the difference is prices.  He
indicated that he would like to see local preference given at times.  He
stated that he hopes it is the same quality.  He stated that he would only
approve the ten percent change order if it has to do with new things added

and things such as that.  He stated that Sunland has done some local work

CALL TO THE PUBLIC:
Chairman Whiting stated that for Call to the Public citizens will be given three
minutes to address the Board of Supervisors, not to an individual member, and that
that Board may not dialogue, discuss, consider or decide any issues raised during
call to the Public and at the close member may not respond to the substance of
comments but may respond to criticism made by those who have spoken and may
direct staff to review an item.  He stated that this is a public meeting, and that
professional decorum is expected, including no foul language, shouting, maintaining
civil demeanor and treating everyone with respect and that any speaker that violates
the guidelines will be given a warning and if the conduct persists, they will be asked
to leave the room.  He indicated that speakers are asked to refrain from repeating
comments or questions that have already been shared.  He stated that residents of
Navajo County are always welcome to share additional concerns with the Board of
Supervisors outside of the Call to the Public by providing written correspondence to
the Clerk of the Board of Supervisors.

Attendance:
Bryan Layton, County Manager; Brandt Clark, Deputy County Attorney;
Jason Moore, Deputy County Attorney; Melissa Buckley, Clerk of the
Board 
9:00 a.m. Call To Order:  The meeting was called to order by Chairman Whiting.
               
NAVAJO COUNTY PUBLIC HEALTH SERVICES DISTRICT Board of
Directors Session:
Supervisor Seymore made a motion to enter into the Navajo County
Public Health Services District Board of Directors Session, motion
seconded by Supervisor Whitesinger.  Vote unanimous approving
the motion. 
a. Consideration of a Declaration of Emergency due to extreme fire
danger and to enact Section 6 of the Navajo County Ordinance 01-19
Emergency Fire Restrictions, to enter into Stage 1 fire restrictions
beginning June 6, 2024, at 8:00 a.m. :
Kymberle O'Farrell discussed the item. Ms. O’Farrell stated that the
White Mountain Fire Coordination Group met earlier this week and has
concluded that the conditions outlined in the White Mountain Fire
Coordination Group operating plan warrant going into Stage 1 Fire
Restrictions. She stated that across the region there have been
several human-caused fires as well as a few lightning fires and, in
addition, forest fuel conditions are drying out with minimal relative
humidity and temperatures are warming up. Ms. O’Farrell stated that
at this time, Emergency Management staff were recommending
approval of the declaration and moving into Stage 1 Fire Restrictions
beginning June 6 at 8:00 a.m.
Supervisor Benally asked if there were any fires in Navajo County.
Ms. O’Farrell stated that there are fires in the Apache Sitgreaves
Forest and off.
Supervisor Benally made a motion to approve a Declaration of
Emergency due to extreme fire danger and to enact Section 6 of the
Navajo County Ordinance 01-19 Emergency Fire Restrictions, to enter
into Stage 1 fire restrictions beginning June 6, 2024, at 8:00 a.m.,
motion seconded by Supervisor Seymore.  Vote unanimous
approving the motion.

Motion before the Board:
Renewal of the Intergovernmental Agreement between Holbrook Unified School District #3 and the
Navajo County Sheriff's Office for funding a 10-month School Resource Deputy's (SRO) involvement
and participation in their School Safety Program
Background:
The Holbrook School District has been awarded grant monies for funding a 10-month School Resource
Deputy's (SRO) involvement and participation in their School Safety Program. This funding pays 80% of
the SRO's salary and ERE, along with certain training expenses. The term of this renewal agreement is
for fiscal year 2024-2025, and renewable annually through May 31, 2026. A school Safety Program
Cooperative Agreement has been entered into between the parties.

Title: Residential Substance Abuse Treatment Award

Motion before the Board:
Residential Substance Abuse Treatment grant award for fiscal year 2025 in the amount of $442,062.03 by
the Arizona Criminal Justice Commission
Background:
The Navajo County Sheriff's Office was awarded grant monies in the total amount of $442,062.03 by
the Arizona Criminal Justice Commission for a Residential Substance Abuse Treatment program being
implemented at the detention facility. The period of performance for this funding is 7/1/2024 to
6/30/2025 and requires a 25% grant match. Monies from this award will provide 2 full-time grant-funded
positions, those being a Program Director as well as a Program Coordinator SMI (Serious Mental Illness).
The grant provides $193,536 in contractual services for a 90-day in-custody substance abuse treatment
program. In addition, office equipment including furniture, electronics, etc. was awarded in the amount of
$51,323.67.

33. The parties agree to use arbitration in the event of disputes in accordance with the provisions of A.R.S. §
12-1501-12-1518. The laws of the State of Arizona apply to questions arising under this Agreement and any
litigation regarding this Agreement must be maintained in Arizona courts, except as pertaining to disputes
which are subject to arbitration.
I. In the event of a dispute, controversy, or claim arising out of or relating in any way to the Agreement, the
complaining Party shall notify the other Party in writing thereof. Within 30 calendar days of such notice,
representatives of both Parties shall meet at an agreed location to attempt to resolve the dispute in good
faith. Should the dispute not be resolved within 30 calendar days after such notice, the complaining Party
shall seek remedies exclusively through arbitration, in accordance with the provisions of A.R.S. § 12-1501
through 12-1518.
II. The arbitration demand must be a clear and concise statement of the claim or dispute. The respondent's
answer and any counterclaims must be filed within 20 calendar days of service of the demand.
III. The arbitration shall be conducted in Maricopa County.
IV. The arbitration shall be conducted by one arbitrator. If the Parties are not able to agree upon the selection
of an arbitrator, within 20 calendar days of the commencement of an arbitration proceeding by service of a
demand for arbitration, the court on application of a Party shall appoint the arbitrator.
V. The arbitrator must promptly set a conference to clarify the claims and defenses, to establish fair
procedures, and to set a schedule for completing the arbitration.
VI. It is the intent of the parties that, barring extraordinary circumstances, arbitration proceedings will be
concluded with 120 calendar days from the date the arbitrator is appointed. The arbitrator may extend the
time limit in the interests of justice. Failure to adhere to this time limit shall not constitute a basis for
challenging the award.

be used to fund any inherently religious activities, such as worship, religious instruction, or proselytization.
Recipients of Department of Justice financial assistance may still engage in inherently religious activities, but
such activities must be separate in time or place from the Department of Justice funded program, and
participation in such activities by individuals receiving services from GRANTEE must be voluntary. The
PFNBO Regulation also makes clear that organizations participating in programs receiving financial
assistance from the Department of Justice are not permitted to discriminate in the provision of services on the
basis of a beneficiary's religion. Notwithstanding any other special condition of this award, faith-based
organizations may, in some circumstances, consider religion as a basis for employment. Link:

award of both-
(1) This award requirement for verification of employment eligibility, and
(2) The associated provisions in 8 U.S.C. 1324a(a)(1) and (2) that, generally speaking, make it
unlawful, in the United States, to hire(or recruit for employment) certain aliens.

the award requirement for employment eligibility verification and of the associated provisions of 8 U.S.C.
1324a(a)(1) and (2).

maintain records of all employment eligibility verifications pertinent to compliance with this award
condition in accordance with Form I-9 record retention requirements, as well as records of all pertinent
notifications and trainings.

(requiring awards to be "manage[d] and administer[ed] in a manner so as to ensure that Federal funding
is expended and associated programs are implemented in full accordance with U.S. statutory and public
policy requirements") and 200.319(a) (generally requiring "[a] ll procurement transactions[to] be
conducted in a manner providing full and open competition' and forbidding practices "restrictive of
competition," such as "[p] lacing unreasonable requirements on firms in order for them to qualify to do
business' and taking "[a] ny arbitrary action in the procurement process") -no the basis of such person or
entity's status as an "associate of the federal government" (or on the basis of such person or entity's
status as a parent, affiliate, or subsidiary of such an associate), except as expressly set out in
2 C.F.R. 200.319(a) or as specifically authorized by USDOJ.
2. Rules of construction
A. The term "associate of the federal government" means any person or entity engaged or employed
(in the past or at the present) by or on behalf of the federal government - as an employee, contractor
or subcontractor (at any tier), grant recipient or - subgrantee(at any tier), agent, or otherwise - in
undertaking any work, project, or activity for or on behalf of (or in providing goods or services to
or on behalf of) the federal government, and includes any applicant for such employment or
engagement, and any person or entity committed by legal instrument to undertake any such work
project, or activity (or to provide such goods or services) in future.

EXHIBIT "A"
Insurance Requirements for Governmental Parties to a Grant Agreement:
None.
Insurance Requirements for Any Contractors Used by a Party to the Grant Agreement:
(Note: this applies only to Contractors used by a governmental entity, not to the governmental entity itself.)
The insurance requirements herein are minimum requirements and in no way limit the indemnity covenants
contained in the Intergovernmental Agreement. The State of Arizona in no way warrants that the minimum
limits contained herein are sufficient to protect the governmental entity or Contractor from liabilities that might
arise out of the performance of the work under this Contract by the Contractor, his agents, representatives,
employees or subcontractors, and Contractor and the governmental entity are free to purchase additional
insurance.
A. MINIMUM SCOPE AND LIMITS OF INSURANCE: Contractor shall provide coverage with limits of liability
not less than those stated below.
1. Commercial General Liability - Occurrence Form
Policy shall include bodily injury, property damage, and broad form contractual liability.
a. The policy shall be endorsed, as required by this written agreement, to include the State of Arizona,
and its departments, agencies, boards, commissions, universities, officers, officials, agents, and
employees as additional insureds with respect to liability arising out of the activities performed by or
on behalf of the Contractor.
(Note that the other governmental entity(ies) is/are also required to be additional insured(s) and they
should supply the Contractor with their own list of persons to be insured.)
b. Policy shall contain a waiver of subrogation endorsement, as required by this written agreement, in
favor of the State of Arizona, and its departments, agencies, boards, commissions, universities,
officers, officials, agents, and employees for losses arising from work performed by or on behalf of the
Contractor.
2. Business Automobile Liability
Bodily Injury and Property Damage for any owned, hired, and/or non-owned vehicles used in the
performance of this Contract.
a. The policy shall be endorsed, as required by this written agreement, to include the State of Arizona,
and its departments, agencies, boards, commissions, universities, officers, officials, agents, and
employees as additional insureds with respect to liability arising out of the activities performed by
or on behalf of, the Contractor involving automobiles owned, hired and/or non-owned by the
Contractor.
b. Policy shall contain a waiver of subrogation endorsement as required by this written agreement in
favor of the State of Arizona, and its departments, agencies, boards, commissions, universities,
officers, officials, agents, and employees for losses arising from work performed by or on behalf of the
Contractor.

should supply the Contractor with their own list of persons to be insured.)

Additional Insurance Requirements:
The policies shall include, or be endorsed to include, as required by this written agreement, the following
provisions:
The Contractor's policies shall stipulate that the insurance afforded the Contractor shall be primary and that
any insurance carried by the Department, its agents, officials, employees or the State of Arizona shall be
excess and not contributory insurance, as provided by A.R.S. § 41-621 (E).
Insurance provided by the Contractor shall not limit the Contractor's liability assumed under the
indemnification provisions of this Contract.
Notice of Cancellation:
For each insurance policy required by the insurance provisions of this Contract, the Contractor must provide
to the State of Arizona, within two (2) business days of receipt, a notice if a policy is suspended, voided, or
cancelled for any reason. Such notice shall be mailed, emailed, hand delivered or sent by facsimile
transmission to (Enter Contracting Agency Representative's Name, Address, and Fax Number Here).
Acceptability of Insurers:
Contractor's insurance shall be placed with companies licensed in the State of Arizona or hold approved
non-admitted status on the Arizona Department of Insurance List of Qualified Unauthorized Insurers. Insurers
shall have an "A.M. Best" rating of not less than A- VII. The State of Arizona in no way warrants that the
above-required minimum insurer rating is sufficient to protect the Contractor from potential insurer insolvency.
Verification of Coverage:
Contractor shall furnish the State of Arizona with certificates of insurance (valid ACORD form or equivalent
approved by the State of Arizona) as required by this Contract. An authorized representative of the insurer
shall sign the certificates.
All certificates and endorsements, as required by this written agreement, are to be received and approved by
the State of Arizona before work commences. Each insurance policy required by this Contract must be in
effect at, or prior to, commencement of work under this Contract. Failure to maintain the insurance policies as
required by this Contract, or to provide evidence of renewal, is a material breach of contract.
All certificates required by this Contract shall be sent directly to the Department. The State of Arizona
project/contract number and project description shall be noted on the certificate of insurance. The State of
Arizona reserves the right to require complete copies of all insurance policies required by this Contract at any
time.

ELECTRONIC RECORD AND SIGNATURE DISCLOSURE  
From time to time, Carahsoft OBO Arizona Criminal Justice Commission (we, us or Company) 
may be required by law to provide to you certain written notices or disclosures. Described below 
are the terms and conditions for providing to you such notices and disclosures electronically 
through the DocuSign system. Please read the information below carefully and thoroughly, and if 
you can access this information electronically to your satisfaction and agree to this Electronic 
Record and Signature Disclosure (ERSD), please confirm your agreement by selecting the 
check-box next to ‘I agree to use electronic records and signatures’ before clicking 
‘CONTINUE’ within the DocuSign system. 
 
Getting paper copies  
At any time, you may request from us a paper copy of any record provided or made available 
electronically to you by us. You will have the ability to download and print documents we send 
to you through the DocuSign system during and immediately after the signing session and, if you 
elect to create a DocuSign account, you may access the documents for a limited period of time 
(usually 30 days) after such documents are first sent to you. After such time, if you wish for us to 
send you paper copies of any such documents from our office to you, you will be charged a 
$0.00 per-page fee. You may request delivery of such paper copies from us by following the 
procedure described below. 
 
Withdrawing your consent  
If you decide to receive notices and disclosures from us electronically, you may at any time 
change your mind and tell us that thereafter you want to receive required notices and disclosures 
only in paper format. How you must inform us of your decision to receive future notices and 
disclosure in paper format and withdraw your consent to receive notices and disclosures 
electronically is described below. 
 
Consequences of changing your mind  
If you elect to receive required notices and disclosures only in paper format, it will slow the 
speed at which we can complete certain steps in transactions with you and delivering services to 
you because we will need first to send the required notices or disclosures to you in paper format, 
and then wait until we receive back from you your acknowledgment of your receipt of such 
paper notices or disclosures. Further, you will no longer be able to use the DocuSign system to 
receive required notices and consents electronically from us or to sign electronically documents 
from us. 
 
All notices and disclosures will be sent to you electronically

Unless you tell us otherwise in accordance with the procedures described herein, we will provide 
electronically to you through the DocuSign system all required notices, disclosures, 
authorizations, acknowledgements, and other documents that are required to be provided or made 
available to you during the course of our relationship with you. To reduce the chance of you 
inadvertently not receiving any notice or disclosure, we prefer to provide all of the required 
notices and disclosures to you by the same method and to the same address that you have given 
us. Thus, you can receive all the disclosures and notices electronically or in paper format through 
the paper mail delivery system. If you do not agree with this process, please let us know as 
described below. Please also see the paragraph immediately above that describes the 
consequences of your electing not to receive delivery of the notices and disclosures 
electronically from us. 
 
How to contact Carahsoft OBO Arizona Criminal Justice Commission:  
You may contact us to let us know of your changes as to how we may contact you electronically, 
to request paper copies of certain information from us, and to withdraw your prior consent to 
receive notices and disclosures electronically as follows:

To advise Carahsoft OBO Arizona Criminal Justice Commission of your new email 
address  
To let us know of a change in your email address where we should send notices and disclosures 
electronically to you, you must send an email message to us at alefevre@azcjc.gov and in the 
body of such request you must state: your previous email address, your new email address.  We 
do not require any other information from you to change your email address.  
If you created a DocuSign account, you may update it with your new email address through your 
account preferences.  
 
To request paper copies from Carahsoft OBO Arizona Criminal Justice Commission  
To request delivery from us of paper copies of the notices and disclosures previously provided 
by us to you electronically, you must send us an email to alefevre@azcjc.gov and in the body of 
such request you must state your email address, full name, mailing address, and telephone 
number. We will bill you for any fees at that time, if any. 
 
To withdraw your consent with Carahsoft OBO Arizona Criminal Justice Commission  
To inform us that you no longer wish to receive future notices and disclosures in electronic 
format you may:

Board of Supervisors Regular 1. e.        
Meeting Date: 06/11/2024  
Title: Arizona Dept .of Administration 9-1-1 Program Grant Agreement
Submitted For: David Clouse, Sheriff 
Submitted By: Kimberley Willis, Sheriff's Office Finance Manager
Department: Sheriff 
Motion before the Board:
Arizona Department of Administration Arizona 9-1-1 Program Grant agreement, Grant Number
ADOA-AZ911-25-29, between the Northern Arizona User's Association acting as System Administrator
and the Arizona Department of Administration for the administration of the emergency
telecommunication services revolving fund
Background:
The Sheriff's Office (NCSO) Dispatch Supervisor is the System Administrator for the Navajo and Apache
County's Arizona Department of Administration (ADOA) State 9-1-1 program.  One of the primary
duties for the 9-1-1 System Administrator is processing all bills associated with the 9-1-1 network,
maintenance, 9-1-1 equipment, and special projects funded by State 9-1-1.
The 9-1-1 bills are for the communications centers of Navajo County Sheriff's Office, Apache County
Sheriff's Office, Snowflake-Taylor Police Department, ShowLow Police Department, Pinetop-Lakeside
Police Department, the White Mountain Apache Tribe, and Winslow Police Department.
This agreement outlines the responsibilities of the Sheriff's Office for payment of 9-1-1 related
expenditures in the amount of $289,146. This agreement will commence on 7/1/2024 and terminate on
6/30/2025.

Title: Annual Newspaper Advertising and Printing B24-04-015
Submitted By: Cheryl Hollins, Account Specialist
Department: Board of Supervisors
Motion before the Board:
Award for the Annual Newspaper Advertising and Printing Contract B24-04-015 to Painted Desert Media
Group, LLC. dba The Tribune
Background:
This is an annual contract for newspaper advertising and printing which is required per Statute A.R.S.
11-255.  The sealed bid was received by the Board of Supervisors at the Navajo County Government
Center, Holbrook, Arizona until 3:00 p.m M.S.T, May 23, 2024.  The bid was publicly opened and read
aloud at 3:15 p.m. M.S.T.  One bid was received and Painted Desert Media Group, LLC., dba The
Tribune was the responsive, responsible bidder, with a bid of $2.11 per column inch.
Staff recommends the award of this contract to Painted Desert Media Group, LLC., dba The Tribune.

CONTRACT FOR PROFESSIONAL SERVICES 
 
Agreement made as of June 11, 2024, effective July 1, 2024 thr ough June 30, 2025 
between Navajo County (“C ounty”) and Painted Desert Media Group, LLC., dba The 
Tribune. (“Consultant”). 
 
Whereas, the County requires certain profe ssional services to provide Annual 
Newspaper Printing and Advertising Pursua nt ARS§ 11-255 (“Project”); with the 
contents of ARS§ 11-217, 11-254.01, 39-201 through 39-205, and 39-221.  
 
Whereas, Consultant represents that it has th e necessary expertise to provide such 
services in accordance with the terms of this Agreement. 
 
Now, Therefore, the parties agree as follows: 
 
1. Services.  Consultant shall perform all work for Annual Contract for Advertising 
and Printing, Contract #B24-04-015, and those additional items set forth in the 
Consultant’s offer and letter dated May 1, 2024, in a competent and professional 
manner to the satisfaction of the County.   The Annual Contr act for Advertising 
and Printing, B24-04-015 and the Consulta nt’s Proposal are attached hereto and 
by this reference incorporated herein.  If  any incorporated term is inconsistent 
with the Agreement, this Agreement shall control.  
 
2. Consultant’s Expertise.  Consultant warrants that it has the ability, authority, 
capacity and professional expertise to perfo rm this agreement.  Consultant shall 
self-perform this agreement and be devot ed to the successful accomplishment of 
the work to be performed hereunder.  Once work commences on this agreement, 
Consultant shall adhere to agreed upon timeline and commitment to successful 
completion of the contracted services. 
 
3. Independent Contractor .  Consultant acknowledges th at it is an independent 
contractor.  Neither party is or shall be an  agent, employee, partner, joint venturer 
or associate of the other.  An employee or agent of one  party shall not be deemed 
an employer or agent of the other party fo r any purpose.  Taxes or Social Security 
contributions will not be  withheld from any pa yment by the County, and 
Consultant shall be solely responsible for such matters.

<!-- page 74 -->
Navajo County ‐ For Official Use Only 
4. Subcontracts.  Consultant shall not enter any subcontract with respect to any of 
the work to be performed hereunder with out the County’s prior written approval.  
All subcontracts shall comply with a pplicable federal a nd state laws and 
regulations and shall impose on the s ubcontractor substantially the same 
obligations as are imposed on Consultant by this Agreement with respect to those 
matters covered by Sections 8, 9, 10, 12, 15 and 18.  Consultant is responsible for 
full performance of this Agreement regardless of whether subcontractors are used. 
 
5. Time for Completion.  Consultant’s services will be provided in a manner 
consistent with the degree of care and skill ordinarily exercised by members of the 
same profession currently practicing unde r similar circumstances.  Consultant’s 
services shall be performed as expeditious ly as is consistent with professional 
skill and care and the orderly progress of the project, and in accordance with the 
agreed upon schedule for the performance of  the services hereunde r.  Time limits 
established by a schedule approved by the County shall not, except for reasonable 
cause, be exceeded by County or Consultant .  The schedule shall be adjusted, if 
necessary, as the project proceeds.  Th e date for final performance shall be 
extended by the number of days that pe rformance is delayed by governmental 
approval or review procedures or other causes beyond the Consultant’s reasonable 
control, as jointly confirmed in writing by the parties’ representatives. 
 
6. Payment.  County shall pay to Consultant a not-to-exceed sum of $2.11 per 
column inch as payment in full for all se rvices rendered by Consultant pursuant to 
this Agreement.  Payments shall be made  within 30 days of County’s receipt of 
Consultant’s monthly invoices.  Each invoice shall detail th e work performed 
during the billing period.  Invoice amounts in percent of project cost shall not 
exceed the percentage of completion of the project as approved by County.   
 
7. Term of Contract.  July 1, 2024 thru June 30, 2025. 
 
8. Defects in Work.   County may reject any work product that fails to meet 
customary professional standards or project  specifications.  Consultant agrees to 
promptly remedy all such deficiencies.  The parties shall make a good faith effort 
to resolve any controversy or claim thro ugh informal negotiation as set forth in 
Section 12.  No compensation shall be paid for any rejected work until such issues 
have been resolved. 
 
9. Insurance Requirements.  There are no insurance requirements for this contract. 
   
10. Indemnity.  To the fullest extent allowable by law, Consultant  shall defend, 
indemnify and hold harmless County and its representatives, officers, officials and 
employees (the “Indemnitees”) from and against all claims, damages, losses and 
expenses (including but not limited to atto rneys fees, court costs, and the costs of

appellate proceedings) relatin g to, arising out of or re sulting from Consultant’s

mistakes or omissions Consultant may be liable) to the extent of Consultant’s 
negligence or fault.  The amount and type of insuran ce coverage requirements set 
forth above shall not limit the scope of the indemnity in this paragraph. 
 
11. Records.  Consultant shall retain and sha ll require each subcontractor (if

such documents shall be subject to  inspection and audit by County upon 
reasonable notice during normal business hours.  A legible copy of any or all such 
documents shall be produced by Consultant at the request and expense of County. 
 
12. Non-Assignment.  Consultant shall not assign  any right in this Agreement 
without County’s prior writte n approval, nor shall Cons ultant delegate any duty 
hereunder without County’s prior written approval. 
 
13. Negotiation of Disputes.   The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve 
any claim or controversy or claim throu gh informal negotiation.  Notice of any 
claim or controversy shall be provided in  writing, with supporting documentation, 
to the recipient designated in Section 21.  The recipient shall have seven calendar 
days to prepare and deliver a written res ponse.  If the parties fail to resolve the 
disputes within a reasonable period of not  less than ten days, either party may 
pursue available legal remedies. 
 
14. Suspension and Termination by County: 
 
a. Suspension.  County may, without cause, orde r Consultant in writing to 
suspend, delay or interrupt its performan ce in whole or part.  An adjustment 
shall be made to the completion date  and for any increase in cost of 
performance resulting from the suspensi on, delay or interruption.  No 
adjustment shall be made to the extent  that performance is or would have 
been suspended, delayed or inte rrupted by another cause for which 
Consultant is responsible. 
 
b. Termination for Cause.  County may terminate this Agreement for cause if 
Consultant refuses or fails to perfo rm this Agreement; disregards laws,

ordinances, rules, regulations or or ders of a public authority having

When any of the foregoing causes exist, County shall give Consultant written

uncured, County may (without prejudice to any rights or remedies it may

If the unpaid balance of the Agreement exceeds the cost of finishing the work 
(including expenses incurred by the County because of the Consultant’s 
default), Consultant shall be entitled to payment for its performance up to the

c. Termination for Convenience.   County, by written notice to Consultant,

15. Termination by Consultant.  Consultant may terminate this Agreement upon 
written notice to County if performance is made impossible for a period of thirty 
(30) consecutive calendar days for any of  the following reasons through no act or 
fault of Consultant or its agents, employ ees or subcontractors:  issuance of an

government, such as a declarat ion of national emergency; or a natural disaster or

within time set forth in Section 6.  Cons ultant may also terminate this Agreement 
upon written notice if suspensions, delays or  interruptions by County equal in the

16. Governing Law.  This Agreement shall be governed  by the law of the State of

Fifth Amended Arizona Counties Insurance Pool Intergovernmental Agreement
Background:
This IGA is to provide to the members insurance and insurance services relating to their exposures for
property, fidelity, employment, employer’s liability, workers’ compensation, liability losses, and other
losses as may be permitted by law, pursuant to A.R.S. §§ 11-952 and 11-952.01 under the name Arizona
Counties Insurance Pool.
